13 Agent Evaluation

Purpose

Agent stack focus for this chapter.

Why is a completed agent run not enough evidence to justify a release?

Evaluating an autonomous agent on final pass rates or aggregate benchmark scores conceals the failure modes that govern production reliability. A task may pass by serendipity while taking hazardous intermediate actions, or fail narrowly after executing dozens of sound steps, and a scalar score distinguishes neither outcome. The agent’s own report that it finished is the weakest evidence available, and tests that run inside its workspace sit within reach of its edits. Even an honest pass is one draw from a stochastic process. The configuration that repairs a defect today can loop on the identical starting state tomorrow, and a system that succeeds on nine runs in ten fails at least one of eight recurrences of the same task more often than not. A comparison between two configurations inherits all of this noise, so a margin of a few tasks on a benchmark of a hundred can vanish under a second sample. Graders err in both directions, two model judges that agree with each other can share the same blind spots, and a held-out suite consulted for every prompt change quietly becomes training data. Once a candidate reaches live traffic, rolling it back stops new work but does not undo the effects it has already released. A release therefore cannot rest on a completed run. It rests on declared acceptance criteria checked by graders the agent cannot reach, on comparisons sized to the uncertainty they must resolve, on retained traces that keep the rare failures engineers most need to inspect, and on staged exposure that bounds what an undetected regression can do. Each exposure the agent carries enters that evidence: the state a result rests on, the resources its horizon consumed, and the authority it exercised along the way.

Learning Objectives
  • Explain why a task acceptance contract credits a trial only on evidence and keeps pending and unknown verdicts in the count
  • Design hermetic trials and isolated graders, calibrating judges by their false-acceptance rate against independent assessment
  • Compare configurations by paired acceptance intervals, repeated-run consistency, and cost per accepted task, sampling tasks rather than runs
  • Analyze a trace’s critical path and design retention that keeps rare failures within a storage budget
  • Diagnose a failure through a controlled intervention, stating what the result does and does not establish
  • Design staged releases that size canary exposure with sequential stopping rules and independent hard tripwires
  • Evaluate trials, paired comparisons, and release conditions as one evidence record that justifies a scoped decision

13.1 The Evaluation Problem

For the repair agent (lighthouse 1.1), the connection-pool repair has come through its turbulent hour. The replacement worker reconstructed candidate workspace ws_r18, kept the interrupted test operation unresolved rather than guessing its outcome, and accounted for the temporary integration-test environment. Suppose a fresh targeted check then passes on ws_r18 and the environment is released, satisfying the cleanup postcondition. Recovery has done its job, but it leaves open the question the previous chapter ended on, whether the runtime completed the task and whether its recovery policy improved outcomes at an acceptable cost.

↩︎ The recovery of the connection-pool repair, which reconstructs ws_r18 and keeps its test operation unresolved, is established in Mechanism composition.

Evaluation examines the state a result rests on, the resources spent across its horizon, and the authority it exercised.

The runtime mechanisms developed so far govern execution integrity rather than task success. An authority check confirms that each action was permitted by policy, the durable event log records what operations took place, and the recovery protocol ensures that partial side effects were undone or safely handed off. None of these components evaluates whether the resulting code patch actually fixes the bug. Relying on the agent’s own assessment is equally insufficient: self-reporting constitutes the weakest tier of closure evidence (The invariant closure principle). When an agent executes tests inside its own mutable workspace, those tests are fully within reach of its edits. A faulty patch can pass simply because the agent commented out an assertion, altered mock data, or deleted a failing test. Even when an agent leaves tests untouched, a single successful run represents only one sample drawn from a stochastic distribution. The same agent may resolve an incident on one attempt and plunge into an infinite loop on the next under the identical starting state.

Engineering decisions are also comparative. A new configuration, candidate_v2, with an updated system prompt, refined tool schemas, a revised recovery policy, or a different base model, is ready for deployment. The engineering team must decide whether candidate_v2 reliably outperforms the incumbent baseline_v1 across the full distribution of repair tasks the system will encounter.

The base model is only one of those levers, and published results show the others moving acceptance as far. A terminal-task benchmark reports the same models run inside many independently built agent scaffolds, the prompts, tool definitions, and control loop that surround the model (Merrill et al. 2026); figure 1 plots every model evaluated in at least five of them.

The widest row in figure 1 is one model scored between 58 percent and 80 percent across 11 scaffolds, and the median spread across the 16 models is 14 points, against a median reported standard error of about 3 points. The differences are therefore real, not sampling noise, and they come from exactly the components candidate_v2 changes. An evaluation that attributes a score to the base model alone credits the wrong part of the system, which is why the configuration under test is declared in full before any trial runs.

Transforming noisy, individual agent trials into a defensible release decision requires an end-to-end evaluation pipeline. That pipeline begins with an acceptance contract, which specifies precisely what evidence credits a task, which invariants must hold, and how missing or malformed artifacts are penalized. To make trials interpretable and secure, the evaluation system isolates task fixtures behind a protected grading boundary that sits beyond the agent’s reach. Behind this boundary, the grading harness partitions criteria between deterministic mechanical checks (static analysis, invariant assertions, hidden unit tests) and calibrated judgments for open-ended properties.

Because agent behavior is stochastic, moving from single runs to configuration comparison demands statistical rigor. Evaluating candidate_v2 against baseline_v1 requires paired trials on the same sampled tasks and decision metrics that reflect operational reality, including acceptance, consistency over repeated runs, and the cost per accepted task introduced in Reliability and Cost over a Horizon. The same evidence pipeline extends past offline benchmarking. Traces explain why a metric moved and are retained under storage and privacy budgets, postmortems test a suspected failure mechanism through a controlled intervention, and staged canaries gate live traffic exposure. Together, these mechanisms assemble every trial into an evidence-backed release decision for candidate_v2, starting with the acceptance contract for a single trial.

13.2 The Task Acceptance Contract

To evaluate an autonomous agent rigorously, the evaluation framework must establish the complete operational context for each run. Consider an evaluation trial for a software repair task, designated pool_repair_01. The run begins from an immutable initial environment snapshot, pool_fixture_v1, and is evaluated against an explicit criteria specification, repair_accept_v1. The system under test, candidate_v2, defines the complete agent configuration, including the base model, prompt templates, tool definitions, and runtime harness. It names an entire operational system rather than an isolated source code revision or model checkpoint. When this trial executes, it produces a concrete candidate artifact, ws_r19. The recovered workspace ws_r18 of the opening belongs to the earlier, interrupted trial of the same task. ws_r19 is the artifact of a fresh trial started from pool_fixture_v1, and it is the candidate the rest of this chapter evaluates. Every verification check must evaluate those exact bytes, so an earlier passing result for ws_r18 provides evidence only about ws_r18.

A trial is a single end-to-end execution conducted under a declared task, fixture, system configuration, and evaluation protocol. A second execution constitutes a distinct trial, even when all inputs, initial states, and configurations remain identical. Rerunning this single repair ten times yields ten trials of one task rather than ten distinct tasks, a distinction whose statistical cost section 13.5.1 quantifies. Before aggregating results across runs, however, the evaluation harness must apply an unambiguous acceptance rule to determine whether any individual trial succeeded.

13.2.1 Evidence dimensions

An evaluation contract translates the high-level task specification (its expected outcomes, permitted operations, and resource constraints) into concrete verification procedures. It is the five-part task contract of The five-part task specification contract made checkable, with the goal and completion criteria turned into measured properties and the permitted actions turned into compliance checks. Specifically, the contract defines what properties are measured, which artifact versions are examined, which instruments perform the measurement, where the observation boundaries lie, and how the harness handles missing or ambiguous evidence. Rather than treating evaluation as a monolithic pass/fail pipeline where an early fault voids all downstream telemetry, table 1 organizes verification across four parallel dimensions of evidence.

The four dimensions organize where evidence comes from. The definition of an accepted task below names the criteria that evidence must satisfy, and the two line up directly. Task outcome supplies the outcome criteria, proposal validity and execution behavior supply the process criteria, and constraint compliance supplies the resource and residual-duty criteria together with the authority side of process. The same split follows the exposures the agent carries. Checks of the resulting artifact and environment examine the state a result rests on, resource criteria measure what the horizon consumed, and compliance checks examine the authority the trial exercised.

Separating these dimensions prevents intermediate runtime recoverable errors from contaminating the assessment of the final artifact. For instance, if an agent generates a tool invocation with an invalid argument type, a local schema validator can reject the call. If the agent inspects the rejection, corrects the payload, and dispatches a valid request that successfully repairs the bug, an evaluation policy that permits self-correction will accept the trial. Conversely, if a security policy strictly prohibits even the attempt to access a sensitive credential store, that single malformed or unauthorized proposal constitutes an immediate, terminal violation.

Because of these differing rules, the evaluation record must distinguish whether every event was a raw proposal, an admitted request, or an observed state mutation. Maintaining parallel evidence streams also preserves diagnostic value when a trial fails. If an agent overwrites a protected configuration file, the trial is rejected; nevertheless, retaining the test execution logs, elapsed wall-clock time, and cleanup traces explains why the system failed, aiding post-mortem analysis without altering the failure verdict.

Definition 13.1: Accepted task

Accepted task is a task completion credited to a particular trial because the available evidence satisfies its declared outcome, process, resource, and residual-duty criteria at the required evidence level.

  1. Significance: Acceptance defines the denominator for cost efficiency (\(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) in equation 1.2). The compute, token, and infrastructure costs of all rejected or unresolved runs are charged against the accepted trials. Artificially weakening the acceptance contract creates an illusion of efficiency, lowering nominal cost per accepted task without making the underlying system cheaper.
  2. Distinction: Passing a single test harness or verifier is substantially narrower than achieving task acceptance. A trial might pass a targeted regression test yet fail due to resource exhaustion, unauthorized side effects, or leaked background processes. Moreover, task identity refers to the underlying problem specification, whereas trial identity refers to an individual execution attempting that task.
  3. Common pitfall: Crediting a trial based solely on an agent’s self-generated claim of completion, a successful transport status code, or a passing test run executed against a different candidate workspace revision.

13.2.2 Evaluation instruments and coverage

Three instruments supply the evidence for these criteria, each bounded by specific operational limits. Runtime guards and external programmatic checks represent the two halves of invariant closure (principle 1). Runtime guards enforce bounds at the operational boundaries they mediate, such as blocking an unauthorized dispatch or intercepting a disallowed network connection. External checks assess specified artifact properties independently after execution, verifying for example whether the submitted candidate passes a defect reproducer and the required regression suite. Finally, qualitative judgments, whether supplied by human experts or automated evaluator models, assess compliance with an explicit rubric, such as whether a generated technical report accurately explains its supporting evidence. Applying model-based rubrics requires the rigorous calibration developed in section 13.4.

All three instruments obey the coverage rule: an evaluation check establishes only those properties that its scope, input vectors, and underlying assumptions directly support. Isolating an automated grader from agent tampering protects the integrity of its measurements, but it cannot expand its coverage. A test suite can pass completely yet still miss a critical defect if the test cases fail to exercise that execution path.

↩︎ The principle that each verification check answers a narrow question rather than certifying task satisfaction is established in Candidate Sequence Verification.

Definition 13.2: State-delta verification

State-delta verification checks specified properties of an observed change between declared initial and resulting environment states, using measurements tied to the relevant versions and task criteria.

  1. Significance: It distinguishes a claimed repair from an observed artifact or service mutation that meets the selected checks, ensuring the agent produced genuine state changes.
  2. Distinction: Verification relies on scoped, structured comparisons of designated state components rather than naive differential subtraction of entire filesystems or databases. Furthermore, verifying that a state delta satisfies designated checks does not prove total correctness across unmonitored subsystems.
  3. Common pitfall: Treating any non-empty patch or successful tool response as evidence that the intended change occurred without unintended side effects.

The evaluation criterion dictates both the required observation and the appropriate instrument. A read-only analysis task succeeds when the agent produces an empirically supported answer while leaving the persistent environment untouched. Conversely, a software repair task alters persistent files, requiring the harness to inspect execution history alongside final disk states. If an agent executes an unauthorized file deletion and subsequently recreates the file before finishing, the action still violates a policy forbidding unauthorized deletion.

Resource accounting likewise demands precisely defined observation boundaries. The evaluation harness must meter the active execution phase and the finalization phase against distinct budget allowances. Token expenditures, financial costs, and occupied compute resources must remain separate accounting streams rather than being collapsed into a single scalar. Moreover, settled operational charges must be distinguished from outstanding reserved liabilities; halting an evaluation trial at a wall-clock cutoff does not automatically cancel background container instances or release cloud reservations.

↩︎ Bounded-liability admission, which keeps outstanding reservations separate from settled charges, is established in Reservation and settlement.

13.2.3 The four-verdict rule

For every declared criterion in the contract, the evaluation harness must retain the observed result and its supporting telemetry. Each criterion resolves to one of four discrete verdicts: pass when the evidence definitively meets the requirement, fail when it establishes a violation, pending while a required assessment remains actively underway within the observation window, and unknown when necessary evidence is missing or unavailable at the cutoff, accompanied by a structured record explaining why. These four categories function strictly as evaluation verdicts rather than trajectory lifecycle states.

The aggregation logic follows strict short-circuiting rules: an established failure of any mandatory criterion immediately resolves the overall trial verdict to fail, even if other criteria remain pending or unobserved. A trial is accepted if and only if all mandatory criteria achieve a pass. If any required check remains unfulfilled and none have failed, the trial remains pending or unknown. This convention is the four-verdict rule. Its most critical corollary governs every measurement in this chapter: incomplete, timed-out, or unresolvable trials must remain in the denominator under a declared rule, because dropping them changes the underlying population being evaluated. Unless a contract declares otherwise, this chapter applies one default. An unresolved trial stays in the count \(\EvaluationTrialCount\) and is not accepted, so a point estimate of the accepted fraction treats it as a non-acceptance. The report lists unresolved trials separately from established failures and adds the two bounds obtained by resolving every one of them as accepted and every one as failed, which shows how far the open verdicts could still move the result.

Example 13.1: A targeted pass is not a completed evaluation
Problem: The trial submits candidate workspace ws_r19 under repair_accept_v1. The contract requires six conditions: the defect reproducer and designated regression checks must pass, the report must state its supported scope, permitted actions and resource allowances must hold, and temporary resources must be released or transferred under its residual-duty policy. The first three are outcome criteria, and the last three are the process, resource, and residual-duty criteria of the accepted-task definition. Table 2 logs what the harness has observed against each condition so far. At an interim observation checkpoint, can this trial be accepted?

Result: No row of table 2 records a failure, yet the trial cannot be accepted at this interim checkpoint. Its overall verdict remains pending because required regression coverage and final accounting reconciliation have not yet concluded. If the regression suite subsequently reports a failed assertion, the overall verdict immediately transitions to fail, while the unresolved charge remains recorded. If the observation window closes before those results arrive, the uncompleted criteria transition to unknown, a gap that neither the targeted pass nor the accepted handoff can fill.

Systems insight: The evaluation record distinguishes task completion, runtime enforcement, grader coverage, and owned residual work. A favorable result in one operational dimension cannot substitute for missing evidence in another.

The acceptance contract formalizes the necessary conditions for crediting an individual trial. However, the validity of comparative benchmarks across multiple systems depends equally on the stability and isolation of the underlying evaluation harness. A silently updated package dependency, an unpinned base container image, or data leakage that exposes test suites to the model can move benchmark scores by more than the difference being measured without reflecting any genuine improvement in system architecture or agent capability.

13.3 Hermetic Evaluation Gyms

Evaluating whether candidate_v2 outperforms baseline_v1 requires far more than comparing two summary reports. Both systems must execute against the declared task under strictly equivalent starting conditions, and the evaluator must verify every result directly against the artifact generated during that specific trial. If a trial inadvertently inherits a modified source file, a cached dependency, or a lingering background test from a previous run, the observed delta reflects environmental contamination rather than true architectural progress.

An evaluation gym provides the task’s interactive environment, establishes controlled starting conditions, and exposes clean interfaces for capturing trial evidence. Its hermetic boundary explicitly specifies which dependencies are provisioned or simulated and which external network and filesystem interactions are blocked. This boundary serves as an experimental contract. It fixes the declared experimental conditions without requiring either identical model responses or absolute production fidelity. A stochastic environment qualifies as hermetic once every source of non-determinism is declared, bounded, and systematically recorded.

13.3.1 Task fixtures

A task fixture packages the exact initial state, dependencies, and configuration required to instantiate an evaluation task. In an automated repair benchmark, for example, pool_fixture_v1 pins the baseline repository commit, the language toolchain and package versions, the simulated test-service behaviors, and the files the agent is permitted to read or edit. The fixture establishes the baseline conditions under which an agent attempts the task, whereas separate acceptance criteria evaluate the resulting artifact.

Table 3 records the conditions that must accompany the trial.

A rigorous benchmark must also document how tasks are sampled from the broader problem distribution, because repeatedly evaluating a single fixture characterizes only that specific repair workflow (section 13.5). Even when an evaluator fixes the pseudo-random seed of the language model, non-deterministic inference serving (dynamic batching, asynchronous token streaming, and accelerator kernel scheduling) introduces subtle timing variations. The evaluation run must therefore record all accessible execution provenance alongside explicit statements regarding unconstrained sources of entropy.

13.3.2 Controlled interactions

Interactive evaluation measures the closed loop where an agent inspects its environment, parses diagnostic feedback, revises its working candidate, and executes intermediate checks. Static question-answering benchmarks leave these multi-turn reasoning and tool-use dynamics entirely unexercised. Because the fixture mirrors the task domain, its components vary accordingly: a document-analysis fixture locks down static reference documents and vector indices, whereas an interactive browser benchmark configures authentication credentials, pre-populated user sessions, and mutable web-application databases.

Providing authentic command-line and language tools within an isolated sandbox allows the evaluation harness to exercise compilation pipelines, filesystem operations, and sub-process execution directly. For external dependencies, stateful service emulators can track conversational state and state-dependent APIs where downstream responses depend on earlier writes. Prerecorded network traces support a much narrower replay boundary: a recorded interaction remains valid only as long as the agent’s request method, endpoint, arguments, schema versions, and call order match the recorded sequence exactly. If the agent deviates by issuing an unexpected API call or reordering operations, the replay harness cannot simply deliver the next historical record. Under the matching-request rule, the evaluator must either provide a synthetically valid response for the novel path or fail fast by signaling an unsupported interaction.

↩︎ Request matching, which a replay adapter applies before returning any recorded response, is established in Harness regression replay.

In an environment testing automated cloud-resource repair, a mock service that invariably returns immediate HTTP 200 responses fails to evaluate whether the agent can handle dropped connections, timeout retries, or eventual consistency. A fault-injecting fixture instead exercises resilience by selectively dropping packets, introducing synthetic latency, or withholding acknowledgments, exposing real-world distributed systems failure modes.

Engineering an evaluation gym involves a continuous trade-off between fidelity and determinism. Simple deterministic mocks maximize reproducibility and run rapidly, but they routinely omit real-world concurrency, race conditions, partial failures, and subtle error codes. Conversely, deploying full-scale, live service instances yields high behavioral fidelity at the expense of heavy provisioning overhead, slow trial resets, and environmental flakiness. Systems architects must align simulation fidelity with the specific capabilities being claimed. Furthermore, any architectural behaviors intentionally omitted by the fixture must be documented alongside reported metrics, because a perfect success rate against a simplified mock shows competence only within that sanitized world. Finally, testing agents against live production or external third-party APIs requires dedicated authorization and hard resource bounds; prefixing an execution run with the label “benchmark” confers no legal or operational authority to touch production infrastructure.

13.3.3 State reset between trials

The sandbox reset contract defines the baseline boundary for sandboxed execution. Rigorous evaluation imposes an additional invariant: every trial must start from an environment identical to the declared task fixture. Resetting a trial requires terminating all background worker processes, capturing the candidate artifact at a consistent filesystem checkpoint, revoking session-specific credentials, and scrubbing every mutable resource. Simply reverting the Git repository to a clean commit is rarely sufficient: orphan background processes, uncleared message queues, temporary directories (/tmp), and stateful API mocks can easily leak side effects into subsequent runs. If an asynchronous operation initiated during an earlier trial remains in-flight, the hosting container or virtual machine cannot be recycled into the warm pool until that background operation terminates or its target resource is destroyed. Discarding a workspace directory while background network writes are still pending leaves the host environment corrupted.

↩︎ The reset contract that must hold before a pooled sandbox serves a new lease is established in The reset contract.

To maintain high throughput across thousands of evaluation runs, evaluation frameworks rely on copy-on-write (CoW) filesystem overlays and virtual machine snapshots rather than provisioning fresh environments from scratch. When reporting benchmark throughput, system designers should explicitly break down end-to-end latency into container warm-up, snapshot reset, agent execution, queueing delays, and grading. Reporting only sandbox lease acquisition times creates a misleading picture of cluster capacity. Controlled sharing of underlying resources is entirely compatible with sound experimental methodology: immutable compiler caches and base operating system images can be shared safely across sandboxes. Similarly, evaluating an agent against a pre-warmed cache is valid when warm-cache performance is the stated target of the experiment. However, whenever concurrent trials compete for shared host resources such as CPU cycles, memory bandwidth, or host kernel threads, evaluators must measure resource contention directly or randomize trial ordering to prevent queuing artifacts from being mistaken for candidate improvements.

13.3.4 Grader isolation

During iterative problem-solving, visible unit tests provide the agent with intermediate feedback to guide code refinement. In contrast, held-out acceptance tests, the sealed tests of the closure evidence levels, must assess the final submission without turning the test assertions themselves into an optimization target. The evaluation harness must isolate reference solutions, secret test suites, grading scripts, and authoritative score registries completely outside the agent’s execution environment. Furthermore, the grading pipeline must cryptographically bind the evaluation score to the exact artifact hash (for example, the SHA-256 fingerprint of workspace snapshot ws_r19) captured at the declared completion boundary.

Submitted code stays untrusted after the agent process stops. If an evaluation harness imports the candidate package directly into the Python interpreter running the grading assertions, the submitted code can monkey-patch core assertions, manipulate built-in object prototypes, or rewrite test runner hooks. Similarly, if secret test files are mounted into the filesystem where candidate code can inspect directory trees or read file contents, the tests are irrevocably compromised. Mounting files as read-only prevents direct overwrites, but it provides zero protection against exfiltration or inspection.

↩︎ Why code running inside the supervisor’s own interpreter can reach the supervisor’s state is established in The reachable object graph.

A robust grading architecture must enforce two distinct security boundaries: the live agent’s access during task execution and the submitted artifact’s execution reach during evaluation. Figure 2 maps this bifurcated infrastructure: an external trusted controller manages evaluation logic, holds the private test suites, and commits results to a secure database, while candidate code executes inside a strictly sandboxed runner. The controller orchestrates testing over a narrow, well-defined remote procedure call (RPC) interface, strictly enforces a patch-admission policy specifying which repository files the candidate was permitted to touch, and treats all candidate-generated stdout, stderr, and exit codes as untrusted observations.

When repository unit tests require in-process execution alongside submitted code, the test harness itself becomes an explicit attack surface. In these configurations, rigorous evaluation harnesses run automated adversarial probes before grading: synthetic exploit patches attempt to read environment variables, inspect parent memory spaces, override assertion operators, and forge passing exit signals. Passing these containment checks demonstrates resistance against known circumvention techniques, though such verification covers only the specific security perimeters tested.

13.3.5 Evaluation leakage

Evaluation leakage can compromise benchmark integrity either before a trial begins or dynamically during execution. Pre-trial leakage occurs when benchmark solutions contaminate pretraining corpora or when external retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) vector indices index ground-truth patches. Runtime leakage occurs when multi-agent systems or persistent memory stores retain information across successive runs, inadvertently passing solutions from one trial to the next.

Furthermore, an evaluation suite can leak methodologically even when the sandboxes remain completely sterile. Repeatedly refining prompts, tool definitions, or agent architectures based on error analyses of held-out test failures converts that held-out suite into an informal training set.1 Hyperparameter adjustments and prompt changes guided by held-out feedback systematically overfit the evaluation suite, eroding its statistical validity as an unbiased assessment of generalization (Dwork et al. 2015). Maintaining sound evaluation requires tracking end-to-end data provenance for task prompts, gold-standard solutions, unit tests, and trajectory logs. While n-gram filtering and embedding similarity checks help flag potential contamination, falling below an arbitrary similarity threshold never guarantees true task independence; Split Design formalizes the dataset splitting protocols required to enforce this separation.

1 Goodhart’s Law and the Held-Out Suite: The selection pressure that breaks an imperfect check during search (Selection pressure on imperfect checks) acts on an evaluation suite in the same way. A held-out suite used to choose among prompts has become a control target, so its score drifts from measuring the task population toward measuring fit to the suite. The simplest remedy is procedural. Retire a suite into development use once it has steered decisions, and hold a fresh one for the release claim.

Mialon, Grégoire, Clémentine Fourrier, Craig Swift, Thomas Wolf, Yann LeCun, and Thomas Scialom. 2023. “GAIA: A Benchmark for General AI Assistants.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2311.12983.

Three widely adopted benchmarks illustrate how fixtures and criteria interact in practice. SWE-bench presents real-world repository issues and validates generated patches against task-specific test suites (Jimenez et al. 2024). Its evaluation harness checks both FAIL_TO_PASS assertions (verifying that the reported defect is resolved) and PASS_TO_PASS assertions (verifying that existing functionality has not regressed) (Jimenez et al. 2024), grounding correctness in concrete runtime behavior rather than static code similarity. GAIA poses complex, tool-assisted questions where each task specifies a concise, unambiguous ground truth, grading only the normalized final answer against an exact-match target (Mialon et al. 2023). While computationally inexpensive and deterministic, this check treats the agent as a black box, observing nothing about the cost, safety, or validity of the intermediate steps taken to reach the output. WebArena deploys fully functional web applications within isolated environments, evaluating tasks by inspecting the resulting database and application state rather than parsing agent transcripts (Zhou et al. 2024). However, targeted state assertions verify only the specific records they query. If an agent executes an e-commerce purchase successfully but inadvertently deletes unrelated order records along the way, a narrow state query still registers a pass. Comprehensive evaluation therefore requires explicit checks for unintended side effects, illustrating why the constraint-compliance dimension in table 1 demands independent validation.

Checkpoint 13.1: Comparable trials and protected evidence

Once a hermetic gym enforces controlled interactions and protects the evidence collection boundary, the remaining systems challenge centers on grading mechanics: identifying which criteria can be verified through deterministic execution checks and which demand probabilistic or human-in-the-loop validation.

13.4 Grading Trajectories

Two trials of pool_repair_01 can submit byte-identical source files and still differ in acceptance status. For ws_r19, only the targeted check has passed, while another trial of the same patch may already have established the regression and accounting evidence that ws_r19 still awaits. Comparing the patches alone cannot tell the two apart. Grading connects each declared criterion to evidence, records what that evidence establishes, and preserves what remains unknown.

13.4.1 Outcome and process criteria

An outcome criterion concerns the requested result, such as the regression checks against ws_r19; it may still inspect history when history determines the result, because a final resource count cannot establish that no duplicate allocation occurred. Process criteria concern how the trial reached its result, and the evidence that process deserves separate grading comes from reasoning tasks. On math word problems, Uesato et al. (2022) found that outcome and process supervision reached similar final-answer accuracy while process supervision sharply reduced correct answers reached through flawed reasoning, and Lightman et al. (2024) found that process-supervised reward models selected correct solutions more reliably. For an agent, the process also includes actions. Current authority, forbidden disclosures, required approvals, and recovery obligations can be mandatory conditions of acceptance, while other process measurements only diagnose. Tool-call validity, for example, measures the fraction of proposed calls that satisfy their schemas and is silent on whether the chosen calls were useful.

Uesato, Jonathan, Nate Kushman, Ramana Kumar, Francis Song, Noah Siegel, Lisa Wang, Antonia Creswell, Geoffrey Irving, and Irina Higgins. 2022. “Solving Math Word Problems with Process- and Outcome-Based Feedback.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2211.14275, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2211.14275.
Lightman, Hunter, Vineet Kosaraju, Yura Burda, Harri Edwards, Bowen Baker, Teddy Lee, Jan Leike, John Schulman, Ilya Sutskever, and Karl Cobbe. 2024. “Let’s Verify Step by Step.” The Twelfth International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

↩︎ Process-supervised reward models and step-level verifiers are established in Outcome and step-level evidence.

Diagnostic metrics need their own denominators and endpoints. A recovery rate counts episodes that met a stated recovery condition among the eligible episodes observed, reporting unresolved episodes separately; a different next action alone is not a recovery. Partial-credit scores show which checked subgoals were met and leave a failed mandatory condition failed, and turns, tokens, and costs are reported with the task outcomes so that a shorter failed trial never reads as an efficiency gain.

13.4.2 Grader error rates

A check can reject satisfactory work or accept unsatisfactory work. The verification asymmetry (principle 3) separates checking a stated property from establishing that the property captures the task. A grader’s error rates therefore need a reference assessment of whether the criterion actually held; rerunning the grader measures only its consistency. False acceptance is especially consequential when accepted trajectories support release or later training, but the two error directions answer different questions. Let \(\ReferenceAssessedFailure\) denote failure of the specified criterion under an independent reference assessment, and let \(\text{Acc}\) denote acceptance by the grader under study. Then distinguish

\[\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate=\Pr(\text{Acc}\mid \ReferenceAssessedFailure), \qquad \text{Failure among accepted}=\Pr(\ReferenceAssessedFailure\mid \text{Acc}).\]

The false-acceptance rate conditions on reference-assessed failures and measures how often the grader accepted them. Failure among accepted conditions on grader acceptances and measures how often independent assessment found failure among them. A random audit of accepted outputs estimates the second quantity; the first requires reference-assessed failures, including those the grader rejected. Both need stated sampling, enough assessed cases, and an uncertainty estimate. Failure among accepted is the complement of the passing-verdict precision of equation 3.3, \(1-\VerifierPrecision\), with the grader’s acceptance as the passing verdict, so the base-rate argument developed there for candidate checks applies to graders unchanged.

↩︎ The precision of a passing verdict, and its collapse at a low valid rate, is established in Check precision and the candidate pool.

The distinction matters when the system changes, because failure among accepted cases depends on the prevalence and kinds of failures as well as on the grader’s errors. A candidate can expose gaps that were rare in the calibration set, so each candidate’s audit rechecks them rather than borrowing a historical false-acceptance rate.

Calibration cases come from the intended task population, with deliberate coverage of important failure modes, assessed independently against the same versioned rubric. Reference assessors also err, so the set keeps its adjudication procedure and unresolved labels. A set that oversamples failures reports that design, since its unweighted error fraction describes the calibration mix rather than deployment, and cases used to tune the grader stay out of it.

13.4.3 Calibration of qualitative judgment

A report’s adequacy or a repair explanation’s clarity may require judgment rather than an exact test. A large language model (LLM) judge is a model used to score such criteria under a rubric, and the evaluation contract states whether its verdict is a required criterion, a signal for human review, or a diagnostic score. A favorable judgment cannot override another criterion’s failure or turn unavailable evidence into a pass.

Ground the judge in trusted result records and discrete rubric items. For the repair explanation, those items can distinguish describing the patch accurately, identifying which checks ran, and acknowledging unresolved work. Candidate text and artifacts remain untrusted inputs to the grader, not instructions that may revise its rubric, and a tool-assisted judge inherits the same authority boundaries as any other tool user.

Zheng et al. (2023) measured position and verbosity biases in model judges and found the evidence for self-enhancement bias inconclusive, so each judge is tested on its own workload. Pairwise judging declares order swapping, ties, and abstentions before scoring and keeps them in the denominator, and a judge from a different model family is not independent by that fact alone.

For categorical ratings of the same cases, Cohen’s kappa (Cohen 1960) summarizes agreement relative to the raters’ marginal label frequencies:

\[\InterAnnotatorAgreement = \frac{\ObservedAnnotatorAgreement - \ChanceAnnotatorAgreement}{1 - \ChanceAnnotatorAgreement}\]

Here \(\ObservedAnnotatorAgreement\) is the observed agreement fraction, and \(\ChanceAnnotatorAgreement=\sum_\RatingCategory \FirstRaterCategoryFraction \SecondRaterCategoryFraction\) uses each rater’s fraction of labels in category \(\RatingCategory\). When \(\ChanceAnnotatorAgreement=1\), the expression is undefined. High raw agreement can arise from heavily concentrated label frequencies.

If two raters each label 90 percent of cases as passing, independently of what the case contains, they agree on 82 percent of cases by chance alone and \(\InterAnnotatorAgreement\) is 0. Report those frequencies, disagreements, and assessment coverage with kappa; the coefficient measures agreement, not whether either rater is correct.

Because shared mistakes survive high agreement, a grader’s operating policy is set by the errors that matter for its role, and it is recalibrated whenever the rubric, judge, evidence interface, or evaluated population changes.

Checkpoint 13.2: Defend a grading verdict

Grading yields calibrated criterion-level verdicts and diagnostic measurements for individual trials, but those verdicts evaluate only the specific executions observed in the test harness. Even when graders are isolated and judges are calibrated against reference assessments, observing that a candidate passes more trials than a baseline does not establish that it is genuinely superior. Generalizing beyond those observed runs requires decomposing variance across stochastic attempts and diverse tasks, establishing confidence intervals that do not collapse at extreme probabilities, and bounding comparative uncertainty through paired trials.

13.5 Statistical Evaluation Rigor

When an updated agent configuration (candidate_v2) passes several more sampled benchmark tasks than a production baseline (baseline_v1) in an evaluation sweep, engineering teams are often tempted to declare immediate progress. However, that modest margin can easily reflect random sampling noise. Conversely, a seemingly smaller overall improvement can provide decisive evidence of system progress if the evaluation protocol preserves task-level pairings and demonstrates that the candidate resolves difficult failures without regressing on previously solved tasks. Distinguishing meaningful engineering progress from statistical fluctuation requires two foundations: defining the target population of tasks that the benchmark is meant to represent, and decomposing variance across both problem instances and non-deterministic execution runs. Rigorous statistical evaluation attaches uncertainty directly to the comparative delta between configurations rather than treating each system as an isolated, independent score.

13.5.1 Tasks and repeated runs

The acceptance contract already separated a task from a trial. Statistics needs that separation sharpened. A task specifies the evaluation problem (a concrete problem description, an environment fixture such as a container filesystem and database snapshot, and an automated verification contract), and a run is one trial of a configuration on that task. The words run and trial name the same execution; run is used here because the statistics count repetitions of a task. Generating a second candidate patch for a connection-pool defect is another run of that same task, not a new problem instance. Because agentic execution relies on stochastic token sampling, network I/O, and asynchronous tool interactions, outcomes fluctuate across repeated runs of identical tasks even when running at temperature zero or with fixed random seeds, for the serving-path reasons traced in Repetition and replay.

Let \(\AttemptPassProbability_i\) be a fixed system’s probability of acceptance on task \(\TaskIndex\) under the declared execution and grading protocol. Suppose tasks are independently sampled from a specified population, and \(\PerTaskRunCount\) runs are independent conditional on each task. Let \(\EstimatedAcceptanceProbability\) be the task-averaged estimate, the mean over the \(\EvaluationSampleCount\) tasks of each task’s fraction of accepted runs. With the same number of runs per task,

\[\operatorname{Var}(\EstimatedAcceptanceProbability)=\frac{\BetweenTaskStandardDeviation^2}{\EvaluationSampleCount}+\frac{\WithinTaskRunStandardDeviation^2}{\EvaluationSampleCount \PerTaskRunCount},\qquad \BetweenTaskStandardDeviation^2=\operatorname{Var}(\AttemptPassProbability_i),\quad \WithinTaskRunStandardDeviation^2=\mathbb E[\AttemptPassProbability_i(1-\AttemptPassProbability_i)]. \tag{1}\]

The first term in equation 1 reflects population diversity in task difficulty (\(\BetweenTaskStandardDeviation^2\)); the second reflects runtime execution variance across repeated attempts on the same task (\(\WithinTaskRunStandardDeviation^2\)). This decomposition provides a concrete operational rule for budgeting evaluation compute. Adding more repeated runs (\(\PerTaskRunCount\)) reduces only the second term. In contrast, evaluating additional independently sampled tasks (\(\EvaluationSampleCount\)) reduces both terms simultaneously. If new tasks cost nothing beyond their runs, a graded run spent on a new task therefore never buys less precision than one spent on a repeat, and the best design runs each task once. Repeats earn a place only because a new task usually costs more than a run, since it needs its own fixture, criteria, and validation. The variance ratio \(\WithinTaskRunStandardDeviation^2/\BetweenTaskStandardDeviation^2\) sets the scale of that trade. Once the number of repeats \(\PerTaskRunCount\) exceeds it, the run term is the smaller of the two, so further repeats shave only that term while the task term, which only fresh tasks reduce, dominates the uncertainty. How far past the ratio repeats remain worthwhile depends on how much a fresh task costs, the trade worked through in the first problem of section 13.14.

Evaluating a fixed benchmark suite targets a narrower objective: estimating mean performance on that specific static collection of problems. Running repeated trials on that fixed suite pins down its sample mean with higher precision, but it does not make the suite any more representative of future, unseen workloads. Furthermore, evaluation suites frequently violate the assumption of independent tasks. Multiple test cases derived from the same source code repository, shared external service degradation during evaluation sweeps, or cross-trial state leakage inside shared sandbox environments all introduce correlated outcomes. Sound statistical analysis must group these evaluations into clusters rather than treating every individual trial as an independent random sample.

13.5.2 Confidence intervals for acceptance

When an evaluation protocol executes a single run across each of \(\EvaluationSampleCount\) independently sampled tasks and observes \(\AcceptedSampleCount\) acceptances, the empirical proportion \(\EstimatedAcceptanceProbability=\AcceptedSampleCount/\EvaluationSampleCount\), the \(\PerTaskRunCount=1\) case of the task-averaged estimate, estimates the true population acceptance probability. A confidence interval quantifies the uncertainty arising from this finite sample size. It does not, however, account for grader error, unhandled task timeouts, test-set contamination, or shifts in the underlying task distribution, which are systemic failure modes that demand independent operational controls.

The standard Wald interval taught in introductory statistics, \(\EstimatedAcceptanceProbability\pm \ConfidenceCriticalQuantile\sqrt{\EstimatedAcceptanceProbability(1-\EstimatedAcceptanceProbability)/\EvaluationSampleCount}\), breaks down in the extreme-probability regimes characteristic of safety and reliability gating. When every observed run succeeds (or every safety check passes), the empirical variance collapses to zero, producing a degenerate zero-width interval that falsely claims absolute certainty. Near the boundaries, the Wald formula can even produce impossible interval endpoints that extend below zero or above one.2 The Wilson score interval (Wilson 1927) avoids both failures by using the hypothesized rate, not the observed one, in the variance:3

2 Wald Interval Coverage: A systematic study of binomial intervals found that the Wald interval’s actual coverage oscillates erratically with both \(\EvaluationSampleCount\) and \(\AcceptanceProbability\), stays poor at sample sizes that textbook rules of thumb call safe, and recommended the Wilson interval for small samples (Brown et al. 2001). Agent evaluation lives in exactly that regime: suites of tens of tasks, and violation rates near zero, where a nominal 95 percent Wald interval can promise far more than it delivers.

Brown, Lawrence D., T. Tony Cai, and Anirban DasGupta. 2001. “Interval Estimation for a Binomial Proportion.” Statistical Science 16 (2): 101–33. https://doi.org/10.1214/ss/1009213286.

3 Wilson score inversion: At two-sided confidence level \(1-\StatisticalSignificanceLevel\), the score interval contains the values \(\AcceptanceProbability\in[0,1]\) satisfying \(\EvaluationSampleCount(\EstimatedAcceptanceProbability-\AcceptanceProbability)^2\le \ConfidenceCriticalQuantile^2\AcceptanceProbability(1-\AcceptanceProbability)\). Solving this quadratic inequality gives the endpoints: the variance uses the hypothesized \(\AcceptanceProbability\), rather than fixing it at \(\EstimatedAcceptanceProbability\) as in the Wald construction (Guthrie 2020, sec. 7.2.4.1).

Guthrie, William F. 2020. NIST/SEMATECH e-Handbook of Statistical Methods (NIST Handbook 151). National Institute of Standards and Technology. https://doi.org/10.18434/M32189.

\[\WilsonIntervalCenter=\frac{\AcceptedSampleCount+\ConfidenceCriticalQuantile^2/2}{\EvaluationSampleCount+\ConfidenceCriticalQuantile^2},\qquad [\AcceptanceIntervalLowerLimit,\AcceptanceIntervalUpperLimit]=\WilsonIntervalCenter\pm\frac{\ConfidenceCriticalQuantile}{\EvaluationSampleCount+\ConfidenceCriticalQuantile^2}\sqrt{\frac{\AcceptedSampleCount(\EvaluationSampleCount-\AcceptedSampleCount)}{\EvaluationSampleCount}+\frac{\ConfidenceCriticalQuantile^2}{4}}. \tag{2}\]

Here \(\ConfidenceCriticalQuantile\) is the standard normal quantile for the selected two-sided confidence level.

For zero acceptances in 50 independent tasks, equation 2 gives a lower limit of zero and an upper limit of \(\ConfidenceCriticalQuantile^2/(50+\ConfidenceCriticalQuantile^2)\), which panel B of figure 4 marks. Read with the outcomes reversed, the same arithmetic prices a clean record: zero observed violations in 50 tasks remains consistent with a violation rate up to that limit.

These binary examples assume complete verdicts. If an evaluation sweep leaves trials unresolved due to timeouts, infrastructure failures, or grader crashes, the evaluation team must prespecify a handling protocol before examining the data, such as the default of section 13.2.3 that counts them as not accepted and reports both-outcome bounds. Simply discarding uncompleted tasks introduces severe survivorship bias, skewing results toward trivial tasks that execute rapidly and pass without contention.

Published leaderboards show how quickly intervals of this kind swallow a ranking. Figure 3 plots one evaluator’s runs of 33 models on a repository-repair benchmark (Jimenez et al. 2024), all under the same scaffold, with the standard error reported for each run (Epoch AI 2026).

With standard errors near two points, two systems need a gap of about 5 points before independent intervals separate them, and 24 of the 24 neighboring pairs fall short of it. A leaderboard position is therefore a weak signal for the release decision this chapter builds toward. The paired design developed next does better on the same budget, because it compares the two systems task by task and cancels the difficulty variance that independent intervals must carry.

13.5.3 Paired outcome comparison

Comparing system versions requires a declared task population, comparable trials, and uncertainty suited to the design, the statistical half of causal and statistical release evidence (principle 12). To compare two systems fairly, run the baseline and candidate on separate, comparably initialized executions of each sampled task, with matched criteria and allowances, and keep each task’s pair of outcomes together. Table 4 organizes a hypothetical complete comparison drawn from a prespecified cohort that never included the pending ws_r19 trial.

The baseline’s accepted count is \(\PairedOutcomeCellCount_{11}+\BaselineOnlyAcceptedTaskCount\) and the candidate’s is \(\PairedOutcomeCellCount_{11}+\CandidateOnlyAcceptedTaskCount\). The observed difference is

\[\EstimatedPairedAcceptanceDifference=\CandidateAcceptanceEstimate-\BaselineAcceptanceEstimate=\frac{\CandidateOnlyAcceptedTaskCount-\BaselineOnlyAcceptedTaskCount}{\EvaluationSampleCount}.\]

The discordant pairs determine the net gain, while the concordant pairs still count in \(\EvaluationSampleCount\) and in the uncertainty. Marginal counts alone cannot reveal whether the candidate gained tasks without losing any or traded many wins for many losses.

McNemar’s test4 (McNemar 1947) asks whether the paired marginal probabilities are equal. Under that null, and conditional on \(\DiscordantPairCount=\BaselineOnlyAcceptedTaskCount+\CandidateOnlyAcceptedTaskCount>0\) discordant pairs, candidate wins are \(\operatorname{Binomial}(\DiscordantPairCount,1/2)\), and the exact two-sided test sums the symmetric tails:

4 McNemar’s Test: The test was introduced for psychological measurements in which the same subjects are classified twice, so the two proportions are correlated and cannot be compared as independent samples. Its central move is to discard concordant pairs from the test statistic, since a task both configurations pass, or both fail, says nothing about which is better. The interval for \(\PairedAcceptanceDifference\) still uses those pairs, because they set the precision of the estimate.

\[\ObservedSignificanceLevel=\min\!\left(1,\;2\sum_{j=0}^{\min(\BaselineOnlyAcceptedTaskCount,\CandidateOnlyAcceptedTaskCount)}\binom{\DiscordantPairCount}{\CandidateWinCount}2^{-\DiscordantPairCount}\right).\]

A release decision usually asks a magnitude question instead of an equality question. Noninferiority is the claim that the candidate loses no more than a declared tolerance \(\NoninferiorityTolerance\), and it needs an interval \([\PairedDifferenceLowerLimit,\PairedDifferenceUpperLimit]\) for \(\PairedAcceptanceDifference\) itself. The worksheet uses Newcombe’s paired-score method 10 (Newcombe 1998), which combines the candidate and baseline Wilson intervals of equation 2 through a continuity-corrected estimate of their within-pair correlation, so that positive within-pair dependence can narrow the interval even when both marginal counts stay fixed. The result is nominal and approximate, valid for one prespecified look under independent paired sampling. Interval for a paired difference of proportions states the interval in full, and the paired bootstrap (Efron 1979) serves statistics that lack such an analytic interval.

Newcombe, Robert G. 1998. “Improved Confidence Intervals for the Difference Between Binomial Proportions Based on Paired Data.” Statistics in Medicine 17 (22): 2635–50. https://doi.org/10.1002/(SICI)1097-0258(19981130)17:22<2635::AID-SIM954>3.0.CO;2-C.

Napkin Math 13.1: A paired gain and an inconclusive gate
Problem: For the 150 task pairs in table 4, does the candidate establish noninferiority under a prespecified tolerance of 2 percentage points, that is, \(\PairedDifferenceLowerLimit>-\NoninferiorityTolerance\) for the lower endpoint of a nominal 95 percent paired-score interval? If not, how many pairs would the gate need?

Math: The observed gain is \(\EstimatedPairedAcceptanceDifference=(\CandidateOnlyAcceptedTaskCount-\BaselineOnlyAcceptedTaskCount)/\EvaluationSampleCount=\) (18 \(-\) 12)/150, or 4 points. Method 10 gives the interval from -3.21 to 11.12 points, so the lower half-width \(\EstimatedPairedAcceptanceDifference-\PairedDifferenceLowerLimit\) is 7.21 points. Clearing the gate needs that half-width below \(\EstimatedPairedAcceptanceDifference+\NoninferiorityTolerance\), 6 points. Half-widths shrink as \(1/\sqrt \EvaluationSampleCount\), so the required count is \(\RequiredTaskPairCount\approx \EvaluationSampleCount\left[(\EstimatedPairedAcceptanceDifference-\PairedDifferenceLowerLimit)/(\EstimatedPairedAcceptanceDifference+\NoninferiorityTolerance)\right]^2\).

Result: The lower endpoint lies below \(-\NoninferiorityTolerance\), so the gate is not met, and the interval includes zero, so superiority is not established either. The exact two-sided McNemar value, 0.362, answers the separate equality question.

Systems insight: The candidate is held, and the knob that moves the decision is the planned number of pairs. A 4-point gain that holds needs about 217 pairs, a count that only centers the expected lower limit on the margin and so clears the gate with roughly even odds. A powered plan adds the power quantile to the confidence quantile while keeping the engineering tolerance fixed, using the paired variance developed in this section. A true gain of zero needs the half-width inside the 2-point tolerance itself, about 1,900 pairs. Plan that count before collecting data, because rerunning the same comparison until one look passes is a different, uncalibrated test.

These planning counts follow from fundamental scaling laws. For a single proportion, the confidence interval half-width scales as \(\ConfidenceCriticalQuantile\sqrt{\AcceptanceProbability(1-\AcceptanceProbability)/\EvaluationSampleCount}\), reaching its maximum at \(\AcceptanceProbability=0.5\). Because precision improves only as \(1/\sqrt{\EvaluationSampleCount}\), halving the margin of error requires a fourfold increase in evaluation tasks. Detecting a difference between two configurations is considerably more demanding than estimating a single rate. For two independent arms with average acceptance probability \(\PooledAcceptanceProbability\), minimum detectable difference \(\MinimumDetectableEffect\), two-sided significance \(\StatisticalSignificanceLevel\), and power \(1-\HypothesisTestFalseNegativeRate\), a standard planning estimate of the tasks needed per arm is

\[\TasksPerComparisonArm\approx\frac{2\,(\ConfidenceCriticalQuantile+\PowerCriticalQuantile)^2\,\PooledAcceptanceProbability(1-\PooledAcceptanceProbability)}{\MinimumDetectableEffect^2}. \tag{3}\]

Statistical planning and bootstrap intervals derives the fuller equal-arm form, which keeps the pooled variance under the null but uses each arm’s own variance under the alternative, and states its assumptions; equation 3 replaces the alternative variance with the pooled one.

At \(\AcceptanceProbability=0.5\) and 95 percent confidence, 100 independent tasks give a half-width of about 9.8 percentage points (the Wilson interval gives 9.6), and a half-width of 2 points needs 2,401 tasks. With a 5 percent two-sided significance target and 80 percent power at \(\PooledAcceptanceProbability=0.5\), equation 3 asks for about 2,450 tasks per arm to detect a 4-point difference and 9,800 per arm for a 2-point difference. The half-width curves in figure 4 flatten long before they reach the 2-point half-width target, and near zero or full acceptance only the Wilson interval stays inside \([0,1]\) with a nonzero width.

Pairing preserves within-task dependence, reducing the variance of the difference when candidate and baseline outcomes have positive covariance. Each task’s candidate-minus-baseline outcome takes the value \(-1\), \(0\), or \(+1\), so with discordant fraction \(\DiscordantPairFraction\) the paired estimate has variance \((\DiscordantPairFraction-\PairedAcceptanceDifference^2)/\EvaluationSampleCount\). The worked table has 20 percent discordant pairs, which puts the paired variance at about 40 percent of the variance two independent arms would have at the same acceptance rates. The same precision therefore needs well under half as many tasks. On the worked table this normal-approximation variance gives a symmetric half-width of about ±7.1 points, which can be computed by hand and sits close to the 7.21-point lower half-width of the asymmetric method-10 interval in notebook 13.1. Estimation, detection, and noninferiority thus need different counts, and a paired plan also rests on an assumed discordance rate that the study must later report as observed.

Pairing the worked comparison leaves about 40 percent of the independent-arm variance.

A powered noninferiority plan uses that paired variance and the distance from the anticipated gain to the negative tolerance. Choose the anticipated gain, discordant fraction, tolerance, and power before collecting the release sample. In a fixed-variance normal approximation, the variance of one candidate-minus-baseline outcome is the discordant fraction minus the squared anticipated gain. Multiply this variance by the squared sum of the confidence and power quantiles, then divide by the squared sum of the anticipated gain and tolerance, which must be positive. For the existing two-sided 95 percent interval, the confidence quantile remains 1.96; adding the power quantile allows for sampling variation that must still leave the estimated lower endpoint above the negative tolerance with the chosen probability. The resulting count is task pairs. The approximation assumes independent pairs, complete verdicts, one planned look, and variance held fixed at its planning value. Rare outcomes, clustering, a changed discordance pattern, or calibration of the actual paired-score gate requires design-specific power assessment; this calculation does not supply exact method-10 power.

The comparison so far uses one run per configuration per task. Permitting multiple attempts changes what success means and what it costs.

13.5.4 Potential and repeated-run consistency

When an agent tackles software repair or multi-step reasoning, producing a candidate pool that contains at least one correct patch, deploying an automated selector that reliably extracts that patch, and operating an agent that solves the problem consistently on every execution represent three fundamentally distinct engineering achievements. Candidate availability under pass@\IndependentAttemptCount defined pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) as candidate availability: the probability that a pool of \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) independent runs contains at least one acceptable solution. For automated workloads that recur continuously in production, such as a support agent answering the same compliance question across thousands of sessions, availability alone is insufficient. Production deployment demands the opposite quantifier: repeated-run consistency, where all \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) independent executions must pass. These two metrics anchor opposite ends of system reliability, and they diverge sharply as \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) scales.

Theorem 13.1: Availability and consistency over repeated runs
For a fixed task \(\TaskIndex\), initial-state distribution, complete generation policy, and binary pass criterion, let runs be independent and identically distributed given the task, each passing with probability \(\AttemptPassProbability_i\). Averaged over the declared task distribution, pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) is the probability that at least one of \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) runs passes, and pass\(^\IndependentAttemptCount\) or repeated-run consistency is the probability that all \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) pass. From \(\PerTaskRunCount_i\ge \IndependentAttemptCount\) fully graded runs with \(\PassingRunCount_i\) passes, pass\(^\IndependentAttemptCount\) has an unbiased subset estimator \(\AllRunsPassEstimate_{i,\IndependentAttemptCount}\) (Yao et al. 2024):

\[\operatorname{pass@}\IndependentAttemptCount=\mathbb{E}_i\left[1-(1-\AttemptPassProbability_i)^\IndependentAttemptCount\right], \tag{4}\]

\[\operatorname{pass}^{\IndependentAttemptCount}=\mathbb{E}_i\left[\AttemptPassProbability_i^\IndependentAttemptCount\right],\qquad \AllRunsPassEstimate_{i,\IndependentAttemptCount}=\frac{\binom{\PassingRunCount_i}{\IndependentAttemptCount}}{\binom{\PerTaskRunCount_i}{\IndependentAttemptCount}}. \tag{5}\]

  • Task pass probability (\(\AttemptPassProbability_i\)): Single-run acceptance under the declared reset and grading protocol.
  • Claim size (\(\IndependentAttemptCount\)): Runs the claim covers, a pool for pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) and recurrences for pass\(^\IndependentAttemptCount\).
  • Graded runs (\(\PerTaskRunCount_i\), \(\PassingRunCount_i\)): Runs generated for the estimate and the number that passed; the numerator of \(\AllRunsPassEstimate_{i,\IndependentAttemptCount}\) is zero when \(\PassingRunCount_i<\IndependentAttemptCount\). The subset estimator for pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) is the one of Candidate availability under pass@\IndependentAttemptCount, applied per task and averaged.

Because pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) rises with \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) while pass\(^\IndependentAttemptCount\) falls, the same system can look near perfect or unreliable depending on which quantifier the claim needs, as notebook 13.2 shows numerically.

5 Consistency on a Live Benchmark: At the benchmark’s 2024 introduction, its authors reported that the strongest function-calling agent they tested completed fewer than 50 percent of tasks, and that its pass\(^\IndependentAttemptCount\) at \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) equal to 8 fell below 25 percent in the retail domain (Yao et al. 2024). The specific agents will age; the pattern will not, because consistency over repeated sessions always sits below single-run success once any task passes with probability below one.

Yao, Shunyu, Noah Shinn, Pedram Razavi, and Karthik Narasimhan. 2024. “\(\tau\)-Bench: A Benchmark for Tool-Agent-User Interaction in Real-World Domains.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2406.12045.

Each \(\IndependentAttemptCount\)-subset of the \(\PerTaskRunCount_i\) graded runs contributes one to \(\AllRunsPassEstimate_{i,\IndependentAttemptCount}\) when all its members pass, so the estimator’s expectation is \(\AttemptPassProbability_i^\IndependentAttemptCount\). It mirrors the pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) estimator, which counts the subsets that contain at least one pass; Pass@\IndependentAttemptCount and repeated-run consistency derives both by indicator averaging and gives product forms that avoid factorial overflow. Average task-level estimates with the declared task weights, never by repeat count. The pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) metric measures candidate availability, while the deployed selector’s returned results are measured separately; pass\(^\IndependentAttemptCount\) can describe complete system runs, selector included. The consistency metric entered agent benchmarking with \(\Glyph{\tau}\)-bench, which reports it for tool-using agents in customer-service domains5 (Yao et al. 2024).

Napkin Math 13.2: Single-run success is not reliability
Problem: A system accepts a recurring task on 90 percent of single runs. How often does it pass all 8 recurrences, and what single-run rate would hold 90 percent consistency?

Math: By equation 5 for one task, pass\(^\IndependentAttemptCount=\AttemptPassProbability^\IndependentAttemptCount\). With \(\AttemptPassProbability\) equal to 0.9 and \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) equal to 8, that is about 43 percent; at 80 percent it falls to about 17 percent. Availability over the same runs, \(1-(1-\AttemptPassProbability)^\IndependentAttemptCount\), is effectively 100 percent. Holding 90 percent requires \(\AttemptPassProbability\ge \TargetRepeatedRunConsistency^{1/\IndependentAttemptCount}\), about 98.7 percent.

Systems insight: Recurrences compound exactly as turns do in equation 1.1, so consistency is set by the single-run success probability raised to the number of recurrences. Halving the single-run failure rate to reach 95 percent lifts pass\(^\IndependentAttemptCount\) only to 66 percent; holding 90 percent needs single-run failure of 1.3 percent, a 7.6× cut. Recurring work therefore buys reliability most cheaply with a check that catches a failed run before delivery, which turns a failed recurrence into a retry.

The stark divergence between near-certain availability and an eight-run consistency of just 43 percent defines the verification gap, the distance between what a pool makes available and what a single unchecked run delivers. A selector paired with a reliable automated checker closes it, converting availability into an accepted delivery at the computational cost priced in section 13.5.5. The same holds for recurring work, where a check before delivery turns a failed recurrence into a retry and the claim becomes selected acceptance, as notebook 13.2 concludes. Recurring work with no check between the run and its consumer, such as an answer a customer reads at once, gets only consistency. The gap is the practical face of the verification asymmetry (principle 3), because the check that closes it is only as trustworthy as its error rates.

At 90 percent per run, availability nears certainty while 8-run consistency falls to 43 percent.

A sequence of \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) independently drawn new tasks has all-success probability \((\mathbb{E}_i[\AttemptPassProbability_i])^\IndependentAttemptCount\). Because \(\FunctionInputVariable^\IndependentAttemptCount\) is convex for \(\IndependentAttemptCount\ge 1\), Jensen’s inequality (the mean of a convex function is at least the function of the mean) places that value at or below \(\mathbb{E}_i[\AttemptPassProbability_i^\IndependentAttemptCount]\), strictly below whenever task difficulty varies across the workload (\(\operatorname{Var}(\AttemptPassProbability_i) > 0\)) and \(\IndependentAttemptCount\ge2\). Consequently, repeating one task overstates consistency across new tasks. Suppose two equally likely task types pass on 50 percent and 90 percent of runs. Repeating one randomly selected task 2 times passes every run 53 percent of the time, while 2 independently drawn tasks all pass only 49 percent of the time. The single-run average is the same, but the experiment is not. Shared outages, state carried between runs, or feedback-conditioned revisions also break these independence formulas, and table 5 names the experiment behind each quantity.

Unresolved verdicts in a pool follow the default of the four-verdict rule, counted as not accepted and reported with the bounds from resolving them all either way. Which row of table 5 a claim needs depends on how the work is used, and the four recurring workloads (lighthouses 1.1, 1.2, 1.3, and 1.4) use it differently, pairing each workload’s checker with the metric and evidence limit in table 6.

Lighthouse 13.1: Evidence standard by archetype

Systems insight: Across the rows of table 6, the archetype decides the quantifier. The repair agent can buy acceptance with checked retries, so it reports selected acceptance priced through \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\). The support agent cannot retry a wrong policy answer once a customer has read it, so its claim is pass\(^\IndependentAttemptCount\) (notebook 13.2). The research agent’s number is only as good as its judge, and the remediation agent’s release rests on hard limits that statistics cannot supply.

13.5.5 Matched-budget comparison

Fix the task distribution, criteria, and resource envelope before comparing policies. An experiment that isolates a prompt change holds the rest of the configuration fixed; one that compares complete policies lets each spend the same envelope differently among generation, checking, and repair, so equal budgets need not mean equal sample counts. Report allowances and actual use, with time and tokens separate from monetary charges, since leaderboards that rank accuracy alone reward needlessly costly agent designs (Kapoor et al. 2024).

For a fixed complete policy \(\pi\), let \(\text{Acc}_\pi\) indicate accepted completion under the declared criteria, \(\AttemptCost\) its total monetary charge, and \(\CostCeiling\) a monetary ceiling. Its budgeted acceptance is

\[\BudgetedAcceptanceProbability(\CostCeiling)=\Pr(\text{Acc}_\pi=1\ \land\ \AttemptCost\le \CostCeiling).\]

Token and elapsed-time ceilings add separate conditions in their own units. The charge covers generation, grading, failed attempts, discarded work, and required cleanup, with unknown charges counted as outstanding liabilities. Observed cost per accepted task divides the total charge for all initiated trials by their accepted count; its population counterpart is \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) from equation 1.2, with \(\mathbb{E}[\AttemptCost]\) as the attempt cost and \(\Pr(\text{Acc}_\pi=1)\) as \(\AcceptanceProbability\). Because failed trials are charged to accepted ones, the ratio never falls below the mean cost of the successful trials alone, and a cohort with no accepted tasks reports its spend and the zero count.

Candidate availability under pass@\IndependentAttemptCount priced breadth in candidates under the most favorable assumptions: independent, isolated candidates with constant pass probability \(\AttemptPassProbability>0\) and a checker that decides the stipulated criterion exactly. A parallel pool of \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) always spends \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) candidates and reaches availability \(\CandidateAvailabilityProbability_k=1-(1-\AttemptPassProbability)^\IndependentAttemptCount\), while sequential generation that stops at the first pass reaches the same \(\CandidateAvailabilityProbability_k\) after an expected \(\mathbb{E}[\SequentialCandidateCount_k]=\CandidateAvailabilityProbability_k/\AttemptPassProbability\) candidates, and never more than \(\IndependentAttemptCount\). Charging each candidate its generation cost \(\CandidateGenerationCost\) and its checking cost \(\CandidateCheckingCost\), in dollars, and charging every initiated trial against the accepted ones turns those counts into costs per accepted task:

\[\BatchCostPerAcceptedTask=\frac{\IndependentAttemptCount(\CandidateGenerationCost+\CandidateCheckingCost)}{\CandidateAvailabilityProbability_k},\qquad \SequentialCostPerAcceptedTask=\frac{\CandidateGenerationCost+\CandidateCheckingCost}{\AttemptPassProbability}. \tag{6}\]

Pricing generation and checking separately exposes a third policy that candidate counts hide. Generating all candidates first and stopping only the checking early costs \(\IndependentAttemptCount \CandidateGenerationCost+(\CandidateAvailabilityProbability_k/\AttemptPassProbability)\CandidateCheckingCost\) per trial in expectation, because generation already performed stays paid for even when checking stops. The formulas in equation 6 omit latency constraints, checker errors, task heterogeneity, and adaptive feedback.

Napkin Math 13.3: Pricing generation and checking separately
Problem: At the same candidate cap, how much does stopping generation at the first pass save per accepted task once generation and checking carry separate dollar prices?

Assumptions: Use a fixed-task pass probability of 40 percent, at most 3 isolated candidates, generation cost $0.03, and exact checking cost $0.01 per candidate, with no other costs.

Math: Both policies return a passing candidate with probability 78.4 percent. Full batch generation and checking cost $0.12 per initiated trial. Sequential generation and checking start 1.96 candidates on average and cost $0.0784. Either policy’s all-fail path incurs the full batch cost. Charging failed trials to accepted ones gives approximately $0.153 for the batch policy vs. $0.10 for sequential early stopping.

Systems insight: Under sequential stopping, \(\CostPerAcceptedTask=(\CandidateGenerationCost+\CandidateCheckingCost)/\AttemptPassProbability\) whatever the cap, so raising the cap buys acceptance probability at no increase in cost per accepted task, while the full batch here pays a 53 percent premium for the same acceptance. Generating all candidates and stopping only the checks falls between the two, since it pays the batch’s full generation cost but only the sequential policy’s expected checking cost. With generation and checking prices held fixed, the one knob that lowers \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) is the single-candidate pass probability. Doubling \(\AttemptPassProbability\) halves it, to $0.05. An imperfect selector, a deadline, or a shared environment changes these formulas and is measured as part of the policy.

Choosing a generator therefore reduces to comparing \((\CandidateGenerationCost+\CandidateCheckingCost)/\AttemptPassProbability\) across generators, rather than choosing \(\IndependentAttemptCount\). When checking dominates, raising \(\AttemptPassProbability\) upstream wins, because every failed attempt pays for a check.

↩︎ The trade-off between spending a fixed deliberation budget on generating candidates and on checking them is established in Selection and evidence costs.

Checkpoint 13.3: Statistical evidence

These metrics quantify outcomes under a declared experiment, and explaining an individual failure takes different evidence.
