The worked candidate shows the consequence. It accepted more paired tasks than the baseline, yet it stays out of production because its paired interval cannot rule out a loss larger than the declared tolerance. Such a hold is the evaluation harness working as intended, because promotion decisions grounded in inspectable traces and explicit operational criteria can be audited and revised as new data arrive, whereas uncalibrated scorecards obscure production risk.

The quantitative decisions developed across this chapter rely on the mathematical relationships summarized in table 15. Each formulation either sizes the empirical evidence required to support an operational claim or translates that evidence into a scoped promotion decision.

Key Takeaways: Match every evaluation claim to its evidence
  • Acceptance is a contract, not a completion claim: A trial counts as successful only when recorded execution evidence satisfies pre-declared criteria across functional outcomes, operational processes, resource budgets, and residual side effects. Unresolved, crashed, or timed-out runs must remain in the evaluation denominator. Dropping inconclusive tasks artificially alters the target population and deceptively deflates the reported effective cost per accepted task (\(\CostPerAcceptedTask\)) without reducing actual system expenditure.
  • A grader establishes only what it covers: Isolated test fixtures and secured grading boundaries prevent an untrusted agent artifact from manipulating its own evaluation harness, yet any automated check validates only the specific invariants it exercises. Furthermore, strong agreement between automated or human judges reflects rater consistency rather than ground-truth correctness. Detecting false acceptances requires calibrating automated graders against verified reference evaluations.
  • Tasks carry the evidence; pairing multiplies it: Once repeats exceed the variance ratio \(\WithinTaskRunStandardDeviation^2/\BetweenTaskStandardDeviation^2\), the between-task component dominates. Only new tasks reduce that component, but fixture cost still enters the allocation; repeats continue to reduce the run component. Pairing preserves the configurations’ within-task dependence. Positive covariance reduces the difference variance, as in the worked comparison.
  • The deployment policy picks the metric: Availability across a candidate pool (\(\operatorname{pass@}\IndependentAttemptCount\)) fits workflows where an automated checker can retry failed attempts cheaply, whereas recurrence consistency (\(\operatorname{pass}^\IndependentAttemptCount\)) is mandatory when an agent must succeed reliably on every invocation without external intervention. For example, a single-run success rate of 90 percent achieves all 8 consecutive recurrences only 43 percent of the time.
  • Retention decides what diagnosis can see: Uniform head sampling before a run completes systematically misses rare failure modes. Conversely, verdict-aware retention prioritizes intermittent bugs but skews raw telemetry distributions. Population failure rates come from complete accounting records; estimates from diagnostic samples require appropriate inclusion probabilities and reweighting. When debugging, engineers must validate explanatory hypotheses through controlled counterfactual interventions, recognizing that diagnostic conclusions apply strictly to the isolated test fixture rather than the unbounded deployment environment.
  • Canary allocation and monitoring control different harms: Sequential hypothesis testing, such as Wald’s SPRT, sets the expected sample count needed to reach a decision on a measured regression. Reducing the canary allocation primarily extends the time needed to collect that evidence while lowering the exposure rate to unmonitored harms. Unmonitored failure modes bypass statistical canary monitors entirely and require hard operational tripwires. Furthermore, rolling back routing terminates only incoming traffic; repairing external mutations and state corruption requires explicit recovery mechanisms.
  • A release is a decision record, not a scorecard: A production release gate mandates satisfying every declared acceptance criterion simultaneously. Superior performance on task accuracy cannot compensate for regressions in tail latency, budget breaches, or safety violations. Inconclusive trials must never be converted into default approvals, and rigorous experimental protocols require fixing hypotheses, sample sizes, and decision thresholds before collecting evaluation data.

Enforcing causal and statistical release evidence (principle 12) requires an evaluation protocol to specify its task criteria, operational boundaries, and statistical uncertainty explicitly. By tying release gates directly to structured execution traces and paired counterfactual baselines, systems engineers ensure that promotion decisions rest on reproducible evidence with stated limits rather than on summary scorecards.

13.13 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

The statistical foundations of agent evaluation predate electronic computing, emerging from industrial quality control and experimental design. Wilson derived the score interval in 1927 to bound binomial success probabilities reliably even in small sample regimes where normal approximations fail (Wilson 1927). Two decades later, McNemar formulated his matched-pairs test to compare binary outcomes across identical subjects, establishing a rigorous basis for head-to-head model comparisons on fixed benchmark sets without assuming treatment independence (McNemar 1947). During World War II, Abraham Wald developed the sequential probability ratio test (SPRT) to optimize industrial quality inspection, showing that deciding acceptance or rejection dynamically as evidence accumulates minimizes the expected sample size relative to fixed-horizon trials (Wald 1945). For qualitative metrics, Cohen introduced \(\InterAnnotatorAgreement\) to quantify inter-annotator agreement beyond chance (Cohen 1960), while Efron later introduced the non-parametric bootstrap to estimate sampling variance and confidence intervals for arbitrary statistics lacking closed-form analytical distributions (Efron 1979). In 2001, Brown, Cai, and DasGupta demonstrated that the standard Wald interval exhibits erratic coverage oscillations and systematically fails near boundary proportions, even at sample sizes of several hundred, which is why this chapter adopts Wilson’s score interval as the engineering default for small-sample agent evaluation suites (Brown et al. 2001).

Wilson, Edwin B. 1927. “Probable Inference, the Law of Succession, and Statistical Inference.” Journal of the American Statistical Association 22 (158): 209–12. https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1927.10502953.
McNemar, Quinn. 1947. “Note on the Sampling Error of the Difference Between Correlated Proportions or Percentages.” Psychometrika 12 (2): 153–57. https://doi.org/10.1007/BF02295996.
Cohen, Jacob. 1960. “A Coefficient of Agreement for Nominal Scales.” Educational and Psychological Measurement 20 (1): 37–46. https://doi.org/10.1177/001316446002000104.
Efron, Bradley. 1979. “Bootstrap Methods: Another Look at the Jackknife.” The Annals of Statistics 7 (1): 1–26. https://doi.org/10.1214/aos/1176344552.
Lamport, Leslie. 1978. “Time, Clocks, and the Ordering of Events in a Distributed System.” Communications of the ACM 21 (7): 558–65. https://doi.org/10.1145/359545.359563.
Fonseca, Rodrigo, George Porter, Randy H. Katz, Scott Shenker, and Ion Stoica. 2007. “X-Trace: A Pervasive Network Tracing Framework.” 4th USENIX Symposium on Networked Systems Design and Implementation (NSDI), 271–84.
Kohavi, Ron, Roger Longbotham, Dan Sommerfield, and Randal M. Henne. 2009. “Controlled Experiments on the Web: Survey and Practical Guide.” Data Mining and Knowledge Discovery 18 (1): 140–81. https://doi.org/10.1007/s10618-008-0114-1.
Johari, Ramesh, Pete Koomen, Leonid Pekelis, and David Walsh. 2017. “Peeking at A/B Tests: Why It Matters, and What to Do about It.” Proceedings of the 23rd ACM SIGKDD International Conference on Knowledge Discovery and Data Mining, 1517–25. https://doi.org/10.1145/3097983.3097992.

The telemetry required to diagnose multi-turn agent execution draws directly from distributed systems observability. Lamport established that asynchronous events across decoupled components admit only a partial order unless system metadata explicitly captures their causal links (Lamport 1978). Fonseca et al. (2007) extended this principle to modern network architectures with X-Trace, propagating deterministic task contexts across heterogeneous protocol layers and administrative domains to reconstruct complete end-to-end execution paths. Sigelman et al. (2010) subsequently proved in Dapper that distributed tracing could operate continuously at production scale by decoupling span collection from the critical path and employing aggressive head-based sampling to bound runtime overhead to negligible levels. Concurrently, production web services transformed deployment validation into continuous experimentation: Kohavi et al. (2009) codified online controlled experiments with staged canary deployments as standard release gates. When engineering teams began monitoring live experimentation dashboards in real time, Johari et al. (2017) proved that repeated significance testing inflates false-positive rates far above the nominal level, establishing why sequential testing bounds must govern automated canary rollouts. Alongside statistical telemetry, Google’s site reliability engineering practice established the blameless postmortem, institutionalizing structured incident postmortems as essential operational records for diagnosing systemic failures (Beyer et al. 2016).

Evaluation methodologies in machine learning arrived at these same conclusions through hard-won practical failures. Dwork et al. (2015) demonstrated that iteratively consulting a held-out evaluation dataset to guide modeling decisions leaks information and causes adaptive overfitting, establishing formal bounds through differential privacy to preserve dataset reuse. Shortly thereafter, Recht et al. (2019) collected new, independent test sets for ImageNet and observed pervasive accuracy degradation across leading architectures, attributing the drops to subtle shifts between the original and replicated test distributions rather than to adaptive overfitting of the original benchmark. Together, these findings demonstrate why sound evaluation protocols must separate fast development feedback from strictly quarantined held-out benchmarks, while precisely characterizing the underlying task population.

Recht, Benjamin, Rebecca Roelofs, Ludwig Schmidt, and Vaishaal Shankar. 2019. “Do ImageNet Classifiers Generalize to ImageNet?” Proceedings of the 36th International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), 5389–400.
Chen, Mark, Jerry Tworek, Heewoo Jun, Qiming Yuan, Henrique Ponde de Oliveira Pinto, Jared Kaplan, Harri Edwards, et al. 2021. “Evaluating Large Language Models Trained on Code.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2107.03374.
Zheng, Lianmin, Wei-Lin Chiang, Ying Sheng, Siyuan Zhuang, Hao Wu, Yonghao Zhuang, Zi Lin, et al. 2023. “Judging LLM-as-a-Judge with MT-Bench and Chatbot Arena.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 36: 46595–623.
Jimenez, Carlos E., John Yang, Alexander Wettig, Shunyu Yao, Kexin Pei, Ofir Press, and Karthik Narasimhan. 2024. “SWE-Bench: Can Language Models Resolve Real-World GitHub Issues?” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Zhou, Shuyan, Frank F. Xu, Hao Zhu, Xuhui Zhou, Robert Lo, Abishek Sridhar, Xianyi Cheng, et al. 2024. “WebArena: A Realistic Web Environment for Building Autonomous Agents.” Proceedings of the 12th International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

As systems shifted toward autonomous execution, static classification metrics yielded to dynamic task validation. In code synthesis, Chen et al. (2021) introduced the pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) metric alongside an unbiased, low-variance estimator to measure execution correctness across multi-sample generation budgets. When automated foundation models were enlisted as evaluators, Zheng et al. (2023) systematically measured the latent biases of model-based judges, measuring systematic position preferences and verbosity biases while finding the evidence for self-enhancement bias inconclusive. Benchmark design subsequently evolved from conversational question-answering to interactive, stateful sandbox environments: SWE-bench evaluated software engineering agents against verified test suites on real GitHub issues (Jimenez et al. 2024), and WebArena placed web agents in live, multi-service environments evaluated on final system states (Zhou et al. 2024). Finally, recognizing that single trajectory outcomes conceal non-deterministic variance, Yao et al. (2024) introduced Tau-bench to measure agent consistency across repeated trials, while Kapoor et al. (2024) demonstrated that rigorous agent comparisons require benchmarking cost and resource consumption alongside accuracy along empirical Pareto frontiers.

Further reading.

  • Wald, “Sequential Tests of Statistical Hypotheses” (Wald 1945): The original derivation of the sequential probability ratio test, its approximate decision boundaries, and its average sample number, providing the mathematical foundation for staged canary deployments and early-stopping test monitors.
  • Brown, Cai, and DasGupta, “Interval Estimation for a Binomial Proportion” (Brown et al. 2001): A definitive empirical and theoretical analysis demonstrating why the standard Wald interval breaks down across small samples and boundary probabilities, establishing the superiority of the Wilson score interval for agent evaluation suites.
  • Sigelman et al., “Dapper” (Sigelman et al. 2010): The foundational architecture of production distributed tracing under strict overhead budgets, examining the sampling policies and trace contexts that enable fine-grained post-hoc diagnosis.
  • Kohavi, Tang, and Xu, Trustworthy Online Controlled Experiments (Kohavi et al. 2020): An essential practitioner’s guide to randomized experimentation in production systems, covering assignment units, sample-ratio mismatches, and the statistical pitfalls that invalidate live rollout decisions.
  • Dwork et al., “The Reusable Holdout” (Dwork et al. 2015): A concise, rigorous treatment explaining how iterative evaluation on a test set degrades its statistical validity through adaptive overfitting, introducing differential privacy mechanisms for sound reuse.
  • Kapoor et al., “AI Agents That Matter” (Kapoor et al. 2024): A compelling case study in agent benchmark methodology, advocating for reporting inference costs and resource consumption alongside success rates along empirical Pareto frontiers.
Wald, Abraham. 1945. “Sequential Tests of Statistical Hypotheses.” The Annals of Mathematical Statistics 16 (2): 117–86. https://doi.org/10.1214/aoms/1177731118.
Kohavi, Ron, Diane Tang, and Ya Xu. 2020. Trustworthy Online Controlled Experiments: A Practical Guide to A/B Testing. Cambridge University Press. https://doi.org/10.1017/9781108653985.
Dwork, Cynthia, Vitaly Feldman, Moritz Hardt, Toniann Pitassi, Omer Reingold, and Aaron Roth. 2015. “The Reusable Holdout: Preserving Validity in Adaptive Data Analysis.” Science 349 (6248): 636–38. https://doi.org/10.1126/science.aaa9375.
Kapoor, Sayash, Benedikt Stroebl, Zachary S. Siegel, Nitya Nadgir, and Arvind Narayanan. 2024. “AI Agents That Matter.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2407.01502.

13.14 Exercises

Each problem states its constraints and ends in a decision about what the evidence supports or what to collect next. Givens are hypothetical unless a lighthouse profile supplies them, and every interval is a nominal 95 percent interval with \(\IntervalNormalQuantile=1.96\).

1. New tasks or repeats (Basic). A pilot of the repair agent’s evaluation suite estimates a mean task acceptance probability of 0.6 and a between-task variance \(\BetweenTaskStandardDeviation^2=0.08\). (a) Derive \(\WithinTaskRunStandardDeviation^2\) from these two givens using its definition in equation 1, and find the number of runs per task beyond which the run term of the variance falls below the task term. (b) With a budget of 600 graded runs, and new tasks available at no extra cost, compare the standard error of \(\EstimatedAcceptanceProbability\) for 600 tasks run once, 200 tasks run 3 times, and 100 tasks run 6 times, and choose a design. (c) Writing and validating a new hermetic fixture in fact costs as much as 10 graded runs. Within the same budget of 600 run-equivalents, find the runs per task \(\PerTaskRunCount\), each with the largest whole task count \(\EvaluationSampleCount\) it affords, that minimizes \(\operatorname{Var}(\EstimatedAcceptanceProbability)\). Explain why the best design moved, and state which population the resulting estimate describes.

2. A clean record on the replica (Basic). The remediation agent (lighthouse 1.3) runs a candidate configuration on an isolated replica for 60 independently sampled incident tasks, and state-delta checks find no wrong change in any of them. From the lighthouse profile, a wrong change that commits costs $12,000, and the human approval that currently gates every change costs $20 of reviewer time. (a) Compute the Wald and Wilson intervals for the wrong-change rate, and state which one a release record can use. (b) Ignoring any later canary check, price the Wilson upper limit as an expected loss per task and compare it with the approval cost. (c) Find how many consecutive clean replica tasks would bring that expected-loss bound below the approval cost. Decide whether the 60 clean runs justify removing human approval, and name the class of evidence that no count of replica runs can supply.

3. Agreement is not accuracy (Basic). Two model judges, A and B, score 200 research-agent (lighthouse 1.2) reports under the same versioned rubric. Both pass 110 reports, A passes and B fails 10, A fails and B passes 20, and both fail 60. An independent reference assessment of the same reports finds 80 failures, of which B accepted 16, and 120 passing reports, of which B accepted 114. (a) Compute the raw agreement and Cohen’s \(\InterAnnotatorAgreement\) between the judges. (b) Compute B’s false-acceptance rate \(\Pr(\text{Acc}\mid \ReferenceAssessedFailure)\) with its Wilson interval, and the failure fraction among B’s acceptances, \(\Pr(\ReferenceAssessedFailure\mid\text{Acc})\). (c) The release policy admits a judge as an acceptance grader only if the Wilson upper limit on its false-acceptance rate is at most 10 percent. Decide whether B qualifies, and state what a random audit of B-accepted reports alone could and could not establish.

4. Paired evidence for the offline gate (Intermediate). A repair-agent comparison runs one baseline and one candidate trial on each of 200 independently sampled tasks, with complete verdicts: both accepted on 120 tasks, only the baseline on \(\BaselineOnlyAcceptedTaskCount=6\), only the candidate on \(\CandidateOnlyAcceptedTaskCount=16\), and neither on 58. The declared tolerance is \(\NoninferiorityTolerance=2\) percentage points. (a) Compute \(\EstimatedPairedAcceptanceDifference\), the discordant fraction \(\DiscordantPairFraction\), and a normal-approximation paired interval from the variance \((\DiscordantPairFraction-\PairedAcceptanceDifference^2)/\EvaluationSampleCount\). Decide whether the candidate is noninferior and whether it is superior. (b) Compute each configuration’s separate Wilson interval, and show what a team that required non-overlapping intervals would have decided. (c) Compute the ratio of independent-arm variance to paired variance at these acceptance rates, and the number of tasks per independent arm that would match the paired precision. (d) Compute the exact two-sided McNemar value. Explain how it can sit just above 0.05 while the paired interval excludes zero, and state which result the declared gate uses.

5. Choose the generator, not the cap (Intermediate). The repair agent selects sequentially, stopping at the first candidate that passes an exact checker costing \(\CandidateCheckingCost=\$0.04\) per candidate. Generator A passes with \(\AcceptanceProbability=0.3\) at \(\CandidateGenerationCost=\$0.05\) per candidate; generator B passes with \(\AcceptanceProbability=0.5\) at \(\CandidateGenerationCost=\$0.12\). (a) Compute \(\SequentialCostPerAcceptedTask\) for each generator. (b) The task deadline allows at most 4 sequential candidates, and the contract requires availability \(\CandidateAvailabilityProbability_k\ge 0.90\) within that cap. Determine which generator meets it, and the smallest cap generator A would need. (c) A teammate proposes generator A with all candidates generated and checked in parallel at the cap from (b) so that the deadline holds. Compute \(\BatchCostPerAcceptedTask\) for that design and compare it with generator B under sequential selection. (d) The team replaces the checker with the full regression suite at \(\CandidateCheckingCost=\$0.20\). Recompute both sequential costs, find the checking cost at which the two generators tie, and state the design rule the crossover illustrates.

6. Second lighthouse: the support agent’s consistency (Intermediate). The support agent (lighthouse 1.4) answers a refund-policy question correctly on 85 percent of single runs, and the same customer asks it in 5 separate sessions. A wrong answer that a customer has read cannot be retried, and a human handoff costs $4 per session. (a) Compute pass\(^\IndependentAttemptCount\) over the 5 sessions, the single-run rate that would hold 90 percent consistency, and the factor by which the single-run failure rate must fall. For contrast, compute pass@\(3\) at the same single-run rate, the quantity a repair agent with a checker in the loop could use. (b) A policy-grounded model judge now screens each answer before delivery, with \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate=0.20\) and \(\VerifierFalseRejectionRate=0.05\), and every rejected answer goes to a human handoff. Per session, compute the probability that a wrong answer reaches the customer, the handoff probability, and the expected handoff cost; then compute the probability that all 5 sessions deliver no wrong answer. (c) Find the largest \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate\) that holds 90 percent over the 5 sessions, and the expected handoff cost per session at that rate. (d) Contrast the constraint that binds this agent’s evaluation claim with the one that binds the repair agent’s, and state which grader measurement the support agent’s release record must therefore carry.

7. A retention budget for rare failures (Intermediate). A repair fleet runs 20,000 evaluation trials per day, each with a 2 MB (decimal) permitted trace body. At the selection boundary, 3 percent of trials have failed a criterion, 1 percent remain pending or unknown, and the rest are ordinary accepted trials. One failure mode occurs 8 times per week, and downstream diagnostic storage is capped at 1.5 GB per day. (a) Compute the daily volume of full retention and of uniform head sampling at \(\HeadSamplingProbability=2\) percent, and the probability that head sampling keeps no detailed trace of the rare mode in a week. (b) Compute the daily volume of tail selection that keeps every failed and unresolved trial plus a 1 percent random control sample of ordinary trials, and check it against the cap. (c) Design a selection policy within the cap that keeps every failed trial. State the sampling rates for unresolved and ordinary trials, the inclusion probabilities the record must keep, and one change to the trace body that would relax the cap. (d) An analyst reports the fraction of retained traces under the policy of part (b) that failed as the fleet’s failure rate. Compute the reported value, and name the record the failure rate must come from.

8. Postmortem of a wrong refund answer (Advanced). The support agent (lighthouse 1.4) tells a customer that a purchase is refundable within 60 days, while the current policy allows 30. The retained trace holds three records. The raw retrieval result contains two policy passages, the current version and a superseded one, each with an effective date; the delivered context contains both passages with the effective dates removed by the observation adapter; and the answer quotes the superseded passage. The team restores a compatible saved prefix ending just before the retrieval call and runs 40 fresh continuations in each of three branches, holding the model configuration, sampling settings, prompt, and a policy-grounded reference check fixed; every run reaches a known verdict. Branch A keeps the original adapter and yields 18 wrong answers. Branch B delivers the effective dates and yields 3 wrong answers. Branch C removes the superseded passage from the index, so only the current version is retrieved, and yields none. Release policy requires the Wilson upper limit on the support agent’s per-session wrong-answer rate to be at most 2 percent. (a) For each claim, state whether the retained record alone, a regression replay of the captured result without a model call, or only the intervention can establish it: the adapter removes effective dates; the model received both passages; the missing dates contributed to the wrong answer. (b) Compute each branch’s wrong-answer rate with its Wilson interval. Decide whether the data support the claim that delivering effective dates reduces wrong answers from this prefix, and whether 40 runs per branch distinguish branch B from branch C. (c) Compute how many consecutive correct continuations a fix would need before its Wilson upper limit met the release policy, and the corresponding lower confidence bound on five-session consistency from branch C’s upper limit at 40 runs, assuming independent recurrences from this same saved prefix. (d) A teammate reports a fourth branch that delivers the dates and also lowers the sampling temperature to zero, with 40 of 40 correct, and proposes shipping it as the fix. Critique the proposal, choose a corrective change, and write the postmortem record’s lines for established findings, tested change, and remaining questions.

9. Size a canary against a deadline (Advanced). A repair-agent canary monitors acceptance with the SPRT of equation 8, comparing \(\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_0=0.80\) with \(\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_1=0.70\) at design targets \(\StatisticalSignificanceLevel=0.05\) and \(\HypothesisTestFalseNegativeRate=0.10\). Eligible tasks arrive regularly at 40 per hour, each label arrives 2 hours after admission when the regression suite finishes, and under \(\HypothesizedAcceptanceProbability_1\) the planning target is an approximate expected decision time of at most 24 hours from the first admission. Separately, a harm the monitor does not measure occurs in 0.5 percent of candidate tasks, and release policy requires the candidate to run for 7 days at its allocation before any wider release. (a) Compute \(\SprtUpperLogBoundary\), \(\SprtLowerLogBoundary\), \(\SprtExpectedLogIncrement_1\), and the no-overshoot \(\mathbb{E}_1[\SprtStoppingSampleCount]\). (b) Find the smallest allocation \(\CanaryAllocationFraction\) that meets that expected-time target. (c) Compute the expected excess failures from the measured regression at that \(\CanaryAllocationFraction\) and at \(\CanaryAllocationFraction=25\) percent. (d) If the allocation continues for all seven days, compute the expected count of unmeasured harms over the 7-day period at both allocations. Choose \(\CanaryAllocationFraction\) and justify it from parts (b) through (d). Explain why this exposure calculation is not an instruction to keep admitting the candidate after a degradation alarm. (e) A teammate proposes replacing the SPRT with a fixed-sample test rerun after every new label, stopping at the first value below 0.05. Critique the proposal.

10. Critique a release report (Advanced). A team asks to promote a repair-agent candidate with the following evidence. The candidate accepted 78 percent of tasks and the baseline 72 percent, reported as a 6-point gain. The baseline ran on 100 tasks sampled last month, and the candidate on 100 newly sampled tasks. The team dropped 12 candidate trials whose regression suites had not finished, so the candidate accepted 69 of the remaining 88. Report quality counted as accepted when an LLM judge scored it at least 4 of 5, justified by 90 percent raw agreement with a second judge. The failure rate in the release notes was computed from tail-sampled traces. (a) Under the four-verdict rule, bound the candidate’s accepted fraction of its admitted cohort, and state what the 6-point claim becomes. (b) Using equation 3 with \(\PooledAcceptanceProbability=0.75\), a 5 percent two-sided significance target, and 80 percent power, compute the tasks per independent arm needed to detect a 6-point difference. (c) Redesign the comparison as paired. Assuming a discordant fraction \(\DiscordantPairFraction=0.15\), an anticipated gain of 3 points, and \(\NoninferiorityTolerance=2\) points, find the number of task pairs at which the normal-approximation lower limit \(\EstimatedPairedAcceptanceDifference-\IntervalNormalQuantile\sqrt{(\DiscordantPairFraction-\EstimatedPairedAcceptanceDifference^2)/\EvaluationSampleCount}\) clears \(-\NoninferiorityTolerance\) if the gain holds. Contrast this expected-limit-centering count with the fixed-variance normal planning count at 80 percent power, using power quantile 0.84. Both calculations assume the stated gain and discordance; the powered count approximates a normal gate rather than calibrating the exact paired-score interval. (d) Identify the remaining defects in the report and write a defensible protocol: which criteria the automated gate enforces, which measurements the judge must carry, and which conditions the team must declare before collecting data.

What’s Next: From evaluation evidence to curated trajectories
Evaluation determines which of these trajectories should become training data, and which failures call for a change outside the model. Trajectory Curation uses evaluation evidence to guide diagnosis, admission, and corpus design, and its first decision is triage. The truncated-observation failure that the postmortem traced largely to the observation adapter belongs in an interface fix, not in a corpus. A logged success is not automatically a useful demonstration, and a failure is not automatically a policy defect. Preserve criterion and configuration provenance, unresolved evidence, and the separation between development data and held-out evaluation. Curation turns that qualified record into a learning-data decision.
